#6977. Teacher prep programs probed in Education Department’s latest anti-DEI push

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The agency said diversification efforts disregard “merit,” but critics say the programs level the playing field for historically excluded candidates.

The U.S. Department of Education opened a string of investigations into teacher preparation programs on Nov. 13, alleging that they discriminate on the basis of race under Title VI or sex under Title IX.

The investigations mark one of the first times the department's Office for Civil Rights has used civil rights protections to challenge diversification efforts in teacher scholarships, hiring, training and recruitment practices.

The programs at the center of the investigations are aimed at recruiting historically underrepresented groups in the teaching profession, such as men and teachers of color.

“Race, national origin, or sex should not decide who will shape America’s next generation, yet many states and universities seem to prioritize recruiting and training ‘minority’ candidates rather than evaluating candidates based solely on merit,” said Assistant Secretary for Civil Rights Kimberly Richey in a Nov. 13 statement.

“Education agencies and institutions that consider race, national origin, or sex as a means to withhold opportunities in selecting, supporting, and preparing teaching candidates violate federal law and compromise education excellence."

Richey concluded that students should be "judged on their hard work and merit, not prejudged by the color of their skin or their sex.”

EdTrust, a nonprofit that advocates for racial and economic equity in American education, said in a statement to K-12 Dive that the Trump administration is using civil rights protections meant to "dismantle discrimination and expand opportunity" to instead "undermine pathways into teaching for people historically excluded from the profession betrays that purpose."

“The administration’s false divide between ‘merit’ and diversity perpetuates the damaging suggestion that teachers of color are less qualified or less deserving," said Eric Duncan, director of Pre-K-12 policy of EdTrust, in the Nov. 16 statement. "It discounts their skills, expertise, and contributions while ignoring the systemic barriers that have denied talented people opportunities for generations. Scholarships that address those barriers help ensure that family wealth does not determine who can become a teacher."

The Education Department's investigations, Duncan said, "risk turning civil rights enforcement into a tool for preserving the very inequities it should confront.”

CUNY, which houses the NYC Men Teach Program being investigated by the department, said in a statement to K-12 Dive that it was "proud of its role educating the teachers of tomorrow and our programs are open to all students regardless of race, gender or national origin."

According to the city's website, the program was created around 2015 in collaboration with the federal Education Department and then-Mayor Bill de Blasio with the goal of putting an additional 1,000 men of color into the city’s teacher pipeline.

A March 2026 evaluation of the program conducted for the NYC mayor's office by Westat, which provides research services for government agencies, shows that the program contributed over 3,100 new male teachers of color to New York City Public Schools between school years 2015–16 and 2023–24.

The American Association of Colleges for Teacher Education said in a Nov. 16 email to K-12 Dive, “Strengthening and diversifying the educator pipeline is essential to addressing persistent teacher shortages and ensuring every PK-12 student has access to well-prepared, effective teachers. Decades of research confirm that a diverse educator workforce benefits all students academically, socially, and emotionally." AACTE added that it does not comment on active federal inquiries involving specific institutions or state agencies.

While the investigations are some of the first of their kind by OCR, they build on previous efforts by the Trump administration to weed out DEI in teacher preparation programs — especially in the wake of the U.S. Supreme Court’s 2023 ruling in Students for Fair Admissions v. Harvard.

Some of the earliest federal grants terminated in 2025 by the administration were under the Supporting Effective Educator Development Grant Program, the Teacher Quality Partnership Program and the Teacher and School Leader Incentive Program, due to what it said was the promotion of "divisive ideologies". The department admitted in a March court filing that it had cancelled 104 out of 109 active TQP and SEED grants.

The loss of over 100 federal teacher training grants last year caused at least 127 teacher candidates to drop out of their programs and another 259 accepted candidates to not enroll after all, according to survey findings released in October.

Those cuts were preceded by an anti-DEI directive that has since been heavily litigated and was most recently blocked in court in September.

That anti-DEI letter specifically called for agency personnel to review all new grant awards and issued grants to ensure those federal dollars “do not fund discriminatory practices — including in the form of DEI.” The directive added that “Illegal DEI policies and practices can violate both the letter and purpose of Federal civil rights law and conflict with the Department’s policy of prioritizing merit, fairness, and excellence in education.”

The Justice Department in August 2025 also warned districts they could be exposed to legal liability by asking job applicants how their “cultural background informs their teaching,” using recruitment strategies targeting candidates from specific geographic areas or racial backgrounds, and asking job candidates to describe how they overcame obstacles.

Since the Supreme Court’s decision in Students for Fair Admissions v. Harvard, civil rights groups have raised concerns that it would set back educational equity efforts even outside of race-conscious admissions. That decision undid race-conscious policies in college admissions, and the Education Department under Trump has used it to justify scaling back other diversity, equity and inclusion initiatives in education.

Scholarship availability, teacher pipelines and student affinity groups were among the top areas beyond college access that advocates were concerned could be impacted in the wake of the ruling.

#6974. Trump taps DEI opponent to lead DOJ’s civil rights division

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

While at the U.S. Department of Education, Craig Trainor threatened to strip federal funding from schools over their diversity initiatives.

The selection of Trainor, who the Justice Department said is entering the position in “an acting capacity,” signals that the Trump administration isn’t backing away from its heavy crackdown on schools and their diversity initiatives. Trainor is set to replace Harmeet Dhillon, one of several top DOJ officials who stepped down after Democrats seized control of both chambers of Congress in the midterm elections.

Under Dhillon, the department’s civil rights division aggressively pursued schools and states over LGBTQ+ policies — such as transgender student inclusion on sports teams and parental notification of students' gender identity concerns. Additionally, the division has pursued race-related investigations into programs meant to be inclusive of historically marginalized students, and it lifted a number of decades-old desegregation orders.

The Justice Department also joined a lawsuit in February against the Los Angeles Unified School District over a court-ordered desegregation program.

During Dhillon’s tenure, the agency entered an interagency agreement with the Education Department, taking on some civil rights investigation duties that were previously housed within the Education Department's Office for Civil Rights.

Since then, the two agencies rescinded at least seven documents related to English learner and other minority protections on the grounds that they were "not enforceable." Trainor has likewise spearheaded major efforts to stamp out DEI in K-12. While serving as the Education Department’s acting assistant secretary for civil rights, Trainor threatened in February 2025 guidance to pull federal funding from schools over DEI initiatives that it said amounted to "race-based decisionmaking."

The letter said "a school may not use students’ personal essays, writing samples, participation in extracurriculars, or other cues" as a proxy for race in issues "pertaining to admissions, hiring, promotion, compensation, financial aid, scholarships, prizes, administrative support, discipline, housing, graduation ceremonies, and all other aspects of student, academic, and campus life."

Federal judges have struck down the guidance. However, the DOJ released similar sweeping guidance last year that threatens to strip federal grant funding from schools over what the agency deems as illegal DEI. DOJ officials flagged several commonplace practices as potentially unlawful, ranging from requiring job applicants how their “cultural background informs their teaching,” using recruitment strategies targeting candidates from specific geographic areas or racial backgrounds, and asking job candidates to describe how they overcame obstacles.

Trainor pursued similar anti-DEI policies in his most recent appointment as the U.S. Department of Housing and Urban Development’s assistant secretary for fair housing and equal opportunity, a position he was confirmed for by the Senate in October 2025. In that role, Trainor issued guidance warning colleges that affinity housing may violate federal law.

#6973. Supreme Court declines Trump bid to revive DEI research funding cuts

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

A lower court blocked the administration from pulling funding from University of California researchers over project topics it opposed.

Shortly after President Donald Trump retook office early last year, federal agencies began slashing federal research funding en masse, often with little warning or explanation.

The UC researchers sued the administration in June 2025, arguing, in part, that the grant terminations violated their First Amendment rights by targeting their work based on viewpoint.

Agencies such as the National Institutes of Health and the National Science Foundation later confirmed in court documents that they cut UC-affiliated grants if they “expressed, or were presumed to express, viewpoints disfavored by the Administration.”

They said they made decisions via keyword searches for terms like “diversity” and “health equity.” NIH also said it canceled some grants because they had previously been awarded diversity supplements, additional funding meant to support researchers from underrepresented and disadvantaged backgrounds.

The Trump administration took the case to the 9th U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals, which sided with the researchers.

In the Trump administration’s request to the Supreme Court, federal attorneys said that the appeals court’s ruling erroneously “interferes with executive-branch priorities in spending taxpayer funds” and establishes “dangerous precedent that will hamstring every future administration when seeking to subsidize private projects that promote view-points aligned with government policy.”

The high court did not address those arguments this week. Its decision paves the way for UC to have its funding fully restored, a process that is already underway.

For example, Farella Braun + Martel, one of the legal firms representing the plaintiffs, said in September that its lawyers reached a settlement agreement with the Environmental Protection Agency to permanently restore $19 million in canceled UC research grants. A hearing on the motion to approve the agreement is scheduled for next month.

U.S. District Judge F. Dennis Saylor paused the highly contested rule just a day before it would have taken effect.

#6970. Virginia is opting out of the federal scholarship program. Will others follow?

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Gov. Abigail Spanberger’s office confirmed her state is reversing course on participating in the Education Freedom Tax Credit.

Spanberger decided to pivot away from participating in the first nationwide federal school choice tax credit program because it does not appear it would benefit students in Virginia public schools, the governor’s office spokesperson said in a Monday statement.

“Public money should go to public education,” the spokesperson said.

As of Nov. 13, the IRS reported that 31 states — including Virginia — had indicated their desire to participate in the first federal school choice tax credit program available nationwide. The list still excludes New York despite Gov. Kathy Hochul announcing in October that the state would opt in.

With the midterm elections earlier this month resulting in six state governorships flipping from Republican to Democrat in Alaska, Georgia, Iowa, Nevada, Ohio and Texas, it’s possible that more last-minute changes to stop participating in the Education Freedom Tax Credit, such as in Virginia, could unfold over the next few months. All six states with newly elected Democratic governors have already opted into the program.

The Education Freedom Tax Credit program was approved by the Republican-led Congress in July 2025 when it was included in the One Big Beautiful Bill Act and signed into law by President Donald Trump.

The scholarships — generated through taxpayer donations to SGOs — could go toward both private school tuition and public school expenses like tutoring, ed tech and services for students with disabilities.

Supporters tout that the program will expand education opportunities for students. Critics, however, push back that the program is a threat to public school funding and will mostly benefit wealthy families.

The Virginia Education Association, a statewide union of teachers and school support professionals, in a Monday statement praised Spanberger for her decision to step away from the Education Freedom Tax Credit. The union said it estimated that Virginia public schools could have lost $1 billion annually if the state had moved forward in its participation in the program.

“Ample evidence tells us that voucher programs are damaging to public schools and students, draining resources from already underfunded school systems,” said VEA President Carol Bauer in a statement. “VEA members applaud Governor Spanberger’s wisdom in opting the Commonwealth out of the federal voucher program, the future of which is in limbo anyway as a new Congress takes over early next year.”

Meanwhile, Steven Cheeseman, president and CEO of the National Catholic Educational Association, said in a Saturday statement that he was “deeply disappointed” and “frankly shocked” about Virginia’s decision to no longer participate in the federal scholarship tax credit program. He urged Spanberger to reconsider and continue completing the opt-in process.

“This is a stunning step backward for Virginia families. Walking away from this program puts politics ahead of the more than one million students in Virginia public schools who will miss out on support for tutoring, school supplies, educational technology, and after-school care,” Cheeseman said.

Christian Education Network Founder Aaron Baer called Spanberger's decision “potentially illegal.” Baer added that the legal teams at Christian Education Network and Virginia Christian Education Network are “actively evaluating the governor’s actions.”

“Families should have the freedom to choose the education that best meets their children's needs, regardless of their income or ZIP code. Denying them access to these scholarships is a direct attack on that freedom,” Baer said in a Monday statement.

#6968. DHS’ 4-year cap on student visa stays struck down by federal judge

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The law does not grant the agency “such broad and open-ended authority to undermine” the student visa program, the judge wrote.

DHS published the rule in July, arguing it was needed to protect national security and prevent visa fraud. International students would have been able to seek extensions on their visas, but the decision would have been at federal officials’ sole discretion.

The rule would have changed long-standing policy permitting foreign students to stay in the U.S. for as long as it takes to complete their academic studies.

Several higher ed groups and unions, including the Presidents’ Alliance on Higher Education and Immigration and the American Federation of Teachers, sued the agency in August over the regulations.

Saylor handed the plaintiffs an early win when he temporarily blocked the visa cap from taking effect. Writing that they were likely to succeed in their case, the judge flagged that the rule could be abused by the Trump administration.

“It is not difficult to imagine how that system might be abused to punish those whose viewpoints or research are disfavored by the officials making the decision — or, indeed, punish disfavored institutions,” Saylor said in his Sept. 14 order.

He repeated those concerns Monday, describing the student visa process DHS sought to set up as an “unreviewable, completely discretionary renewal system.”

Saylor further said DHS failed to weigh “less burdensome” options.

The DHS rule would have done more than limit student visa stays. Some international students would have been barred from changing their field of study or transferring colleges. And they could not come to the U.S. to pursue an academic degree at the same or a lower level than a degree they already possess.

For example, an international student who holds a law degree would effectively be banned from pursuing a master’s degree in the U.S., Saylor said.

“Put bluntly, DHS is seeking to prevent or discourage the very activity — providing educational opportunities for foreign students in the United States — that Congress sought to permit and encourage,” he said. “The statute simply does not grant DHS such broad and open-ended authority” to undermine the student visa program.

The judge’s decision may not put the case to bed, as the Trump administration could still appeal it. The continued uncertainty comes as new data shows a decline in international enrollment in the U.S.

In 2025-26, the number of foreign students attending U.S. colleges dropped 2.7% year over year, according to the latest Open Doors report. And preliminary data from fall 2026 found that international enrollment declined another 9% annually at surveyed institutions. Both decreases were driven by losses of new students and those pursuing graduate degrees.

#6964. Head of Research at CoinMarketCap

CoinMarketCap is hiring a Head of Research to turn CMC data into research that institutions, media and partners trust and pay for. This is a senior, hands-on role reporting to the CMO.

CMC is the most referenced price and market data source in crypto. Our research already reaches millions each month through reports like our Monthly Exchange Report, Asset Watch and Market Pulse, and is cited by Bloomberg, WSJ, Forbes and Fortune. The next step is different. We are building CMC into a data platform for crypto, tokenized assets and broader markets, and research is how we prove the quality of that data to the people who buy it.

You will own our research agenda end to end: what we publish, how rigorous it is, and how it supports our enterprise data business. You will also be the senior voice on sensitive market analysis for CMC and its partners.

Set and run the research roadmap, from recurring flagships (monthly exchange report, weekly market and asset coverage) to deep dives on exchanges, tokenized assets, RWAs and market structure.

Build institutional grade research that showcases CMC data to banks, funds, data buyers and enterprise API clients, working closely with our API and BD teams on what buyers need.

Own methodology and data integrity: define how we measure volume, liquidity, flows and reserves, document it, and make sure every published number holds up to scrutiny.

Lead research on sensitive or fast moving market events, balancing speed, neutrality and accuracy, and handle review requests from partners with sound judgement.

Be a credible public face for CMC research with tier one media, on panels and in interviews.

Manage and develop a small research team, currently one research analyst, and use AI and automation to scale output without lowering quality.

Partner with marketing, editorial and product to turn research into formats people actually read and share, from long form reports to charts and short social posts.

7+ years in research or data analysis, with at least 3 in crypto or digital asset markets.

A track record of published, data heavy research that institutions or serious media rely on.

Deep understanding of market structure: exchanges, spot and derivatives, liquidity, onchain flows and proof of reserves.

Strong quantitative skills and hands on data work (SQL or Python, APIs, large datasets).

Excellent written English and the judgement to present sensitive findings neutrally and accurately.

Experience leading or mentoring analysts.

Background at a crypto data provider, an institutional research desk or a TradFi data business.

Understanding of how data is packaged and sold to institutions (licensing, enterprise feeds, research subscriptions).

Knowledge of tokenized assets, RWAs or traditional market data.

Existing relationships with tier one financial and crypto media.

Comfort using AI tools to automate data pulls and drafts.

Mandarin, given our close work with Chinese speaking partners and media.

#6963. ABA accreditation arm moves to nix full-time faculty standard for first-year classes

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The U.S. Department of Justice recently threatened an antitrust probe over the requirement, arguing that it drives up legal education costs.

The ABA’s accreditation council voted to eliminate the standard while under pressure from the Justice Department. In an October letter, Associate Attorney General Stanley Woodward said the group could face an antitrust investigation if it didn’t eliminate the full-time faculty requirement, according to The Wall Street Journal.

Woodward argued that adjunct faculty are less expensive to hire and often have more firsthand experience, and that limiting their role in first-year law school education drives up costs and hurts students, the Journal reported.

The accreditation council also plans to seek public comment on further potential changes to its policies regarding full-time instructors that are meant to provide more flexibility to law schools.

The council is in communication with the Justice Department, a spokesperson for the council said Tuesday. The department did not immediately respond to a request for comment Tuesday.

Woodward’s letter came after the ABA’s accreditation council began seeking public comment in September on proposed changes to its full-time faculty requirements.

The accreditation council’s proposals drew mixed reactions from law schools and professors.

In a public comment, the faculty leadership of the University of Colorado Boulder’s law school opposed nixing the requirement regarding the first year of curriculum.

They argued that the first-year of law school “is a key period for educating students on core legal principles, doctrines, and analytical methods” and “a critically important time for creating community among law school students.”

“Full-time faculty are simply more available for answering student questions outside of class, as it is part of their full-time job to do so,” the faculty leaders wrote. “And perhaps most importantly, full-time faculty build pedagogical expertise in designing courses, sequencing learning, assessing progress, and giving constructive feedback.”

Yet others voiced support for the change. Terence Lau, dean of Syracuse University’s law school, argued that the changes would reduce “undue burden” while still preserving full-time faculty members’ “core role” in legal education.

Lau argued the current standard limits the ability for law schools to tap sitting judges or emeritus professors for first-year instruction. “Law schools should be able to draw on these teachers where they best serve students,” he wrote.

Last week’s vote comes as the accreditation council is under the microscope by the Trump administration.

In August, a report from U.S. Department of Education staffers recommended that the agency strip the ABA’s accreditation council of federal recognition. The staffers concluded that the council wasn’t compliant with over two dozen federal criteria and alleged that it wasn’t sufficiently independent from the ABA’s professional organization.

Losing federal recognition would mean the ABA’s accreditation council could no longer serve as a gatekeeper to Title IV financial aid. Although the group accredits some 200 law schools, only five rely on it for access to federal aid, a council representative said in September to a federal advisory panel reviewing the department’s findings.

That panel, which advises the Education Department on accreditation matters, deadlocked on a vote on whether to recommend that the council lose recognition. Education Under Secretary Nicholas Kent is soon expected to make a final call on the matter.

The ABA’s accreditation council has also caught flak from the Trump administration over its prior diversity standard. The council formally repealed the standard in September after suspending it in February 2025.

#6962. Are strict school cellphone bans working?

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Schools requiring devices to be locked in pouches saw gains equal to as much as two weeks of additional instruction per year, a Harvard study finds.

Expanded access to broadband cellphones appeared to occur around the same time student achievement began a slow but steady long-term decline beginning in 2013, the Harvard study said.

For teens, the research noted that access to cellphones grew quickly, from 23% of 13- to 17-year-olds having smartphones in 2011 to 95% by 2018.

Every year since about 2013, U.S. students have lost two to three weeks of learning, said Tom Kane, a Harvard professor and one of the leading authors of the study, in a Tuesday statement.

“It’s been a slow leak that, over time, has put U.S. 8th graders almost a year behind. Strict cell phone bans seem to plug about half of that leak,” Kane said.

This research comes as states increasingly adopt policies to restrict cellphone use in schools and as student test scores on the National Assessment of Educational Progress continue to disappoint, particularly since the COVID-19 pandemic. Twenty-two states require bell-to-bell cellphone bans as of Oct. 29, according to a tracker by the National Council of School Safety Directors.

The Harvard study’s findings differ from another key report by the National Bureau of Economic Research released earlier this year on the impact of cellphone pouches.

That NBER study said there was little effect on academic achievement three years after schools adopted cellphone pouches. Researchers, however, found an 80% drop in students’ reported cellphone use once personal device pouch policies were in place. Part of the NBER study included an analysis of GPS “pings” on cellphones from 40,542 schools between January 2019 and January 2026.

At the same time, a survey shared by The Brookings Institution in September reported that nearly half of teens ages 13-17 said their teachers' enforcement of cellphone restrictions waned over the course of the 2025-26 school year. Researchers from the Center for Applied Research in Education — part of the University of Southern California’s Center for Economic and Social Research — who analyzed the survey findings concluded that bell-to-bell policies may be more durable to implement in schools versus less-strict cellphone policies.

#6960. AI Native Head Of Growth - EdTech (French fluent 🇫🇷) at Paradox

Most growth teams call themselves data-driven. They have a dashboard, an agency and a ChatGPT licence to write ad copy faster. That’s the 99%.

We want to be part of the 0.1%: a company where AI doesn’t just help the growth team, it runs the growth loop. Every day, on every channel.

Then it’s your chance to define a new category : The Operating System (OS) for Human Greatness Augmented with AI.

Science-backed. Design-led. AI-powered. Global.

We’re not looking for someone who only advises on what to test, we’re looking for someone who builds it. A hands-on Head of Growth who can ideate, write the copy, design the strategy and launch experiments directly.

1. Generate hypotheses. Read the signals (ad performance, webinar attendance, sales calls, objections, churn reasons, comments, competitors’ ads) and propose what to test next, with a cost and an expected impact.

2. Act across every tool. Not a chatbot in a tab: it reads and writes in our whole stack, from ad accounts to CRM, landing pages, webinars, email, social DMs and analytics.

3. Hold the full context of Paradox. Our DNA, offers, voice, member stories, past campaigns and what they taught us, in one shared company brain.

4. Ship its hypotheses. Produce the hook, the ad cut, the page, the email sequence or the funnel variant, and put it live.

5. Measure with real KPIs. Every test is tied to a number that matters (cost per qualified lead, show-up rate, close rate, CAC, payback, LTV), not to likes or reach.

6. Learn and loop on its own. Scale what works, kill what doesn’t, write the lesson back into the brain, and launch the next cycle without waiting for a meeting.

This is already real. Our growth hub tracks every hypothesis and experiment, with its result. An ads library of thousands of creatives feeds our creative research. Agents cut ads from long-form content, prepare webinars, wire Instagram comment-to-DM automations and audit our tracking after every live. Athena, our AI coach, works with members every day.

We believe self-help is broken: generic theory, a quick high, no real change.

We believe people deserve more. More than content. More than motivation.

They deserve a system that knows them, one that adapts, challenges, and grows with them.

Growth at Paradox means bringing people into something that actually changes their life, and proving it.

- Human Intelligence (coaches, peers, and mentors)

- Structured Intelligence (protocols, mental models, rituals)

- Technological Intelligence (AI personalization, feedback loops, adaptive journeys)

To create one seamless ecosystem: Paradox OS.

Paradox was founded by David Laroche, a creator followed by over 2 million people and known for his interviews with icons like Tony Parker, Mike Horn, Richard Branson and Gad Elmaleh. We started in French. We’re building for the world.

Here’s the bet: a company that learns by itself grows in a way a company of people alone can’t. A small growth team running a self-learning machine can out-test, out-learn and out-grow teams a hundred times its size.

We hold ourselves to the standards of Apple, Spotify and MasterClass. While others hide their numbers, we let our results speak.

As Head of Growth, you’ll be the architect of a growth machine that learns by itself. In a classic growth team, people do the work and AI helps. Here it’s the other way round: agents do the work, you design the system, set the bar and make the calls.

You don’t just manage a team, you get your hands dirty to identify growth levers and pull them yourself.

❌ The old way: a Head of Growth hires people and agencies, briefs them, and checks their work.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: your team is mostly agents, and you build them yourself. You write their skills, plug them into our tools (ad accounts, CRM, webinars, email, analytics) and write the evals that tell an agent what "good" means: a hook that stops the scroll, a page that converts, a test that’s worth its cost.

You give autonomy step by step: first an agent suggests, then it acts once you approve, then it acts alone within a budget and guardrails. You move each agent up a level only when its track record earns it.

Example: in your first month you build the agent that reviews every ad set overnight. At first it only suggests. By month three it pauses losers and moves budget by itself within limits you set, and you only see what was decided and why.

❌ The old way: a media buyer checks the dashboards each morning, changes bids by hand and waits for the weekly report.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: you build and improve the agents that watch every ad set, page, webinar and email sequence around the clock. They spot what’s dropping, find the cause, fix it or launch a variant, and report what they did.

You give autonomy step by step: an agent can pause an ad on its own long before it can raise a budget. You decide where each agent stops and a human takes over.

Example: show-up rate drops on Tuesday’s webinar. Before anyone notices, an agent has found the cause (a reminder that never went out), fixed it and started testing a new reminder sequence.

❌ The old way: a creative team briefs an agency, waits two weeks and gets five ads.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: you build the agents that turn every live, interview and member story into hooks, ad cuts and shorts, that study what the best advertisers in the world are running, and that write in our voice.

Your taste becomes the eval: you write down what makes a great hook, a great cut and a great story, in rules and examples the agents check themselves against. Each time you reject something, the reason goes back into the rules.

Example: one webinar becomes dozens of hook variants by the next morning. You keep the best few, and next week the agents’ first drafts are already closer to your bar.

❌ The old way: an analyst rebuilds the numbers in a spreadsheet at the end of the month, and nobody trusts them.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: you build the agents that connect every touch to cash, from ad to lead, live, sale and transformed member, and that audit the tracking after every campaign.

No number, no test: you define the KPIs that matter (CAC, show-up, close rate, LTV, payback), and agents refuse to launch a test that isn’t wired to one.

Example: after each live, an agent cross-checks the ad platform, the CRM and our analytics, finds where they disagree and fixes the leak before the next live.

❌ The old way: lessons live in people’s heads and leave with them.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: every result goes back into the company brain, and agents read it before proposing anything. The machine never learns the same lesson twice.

You measure the machine itself: tests per week, time from idea to live, the share of the work done without a human, and how many agents moved up an autonomy level this month. You push those numbers up.

And always: no fake scarcity, no fake claims, real stories told with consent. The machine can scale, but it doesn’t decide what’s right. You do.

How you’ll know you’re AI-native: you spend your time building agents, writing evals and making calls, not doing the work by hand. Most experiments are generated and shipped by agents. The output grows every month while the headcount doesn’t.

You’ll own these KPIs: LTV / CAC ratio, funnel conversion rate by stage, experiments launched per week.

Desired outcomes: conversion rates 2x better than baseline, growth experimentation that runs continuously, a predictable growth engine.

Tools you’ll master: GA4, Optibase, Vidalytics, Clarity, Hotjar, Amplitude, Figma, Looker Studio, VWO, Optimizely, Claude Code, and whatever helps you see what others miss.

You have ideally a minimum of 3 years of experience in Conversion Rate Optimization, Growth Experimentation, or Performance Marketing, ideally within SaaS, e-commerce, or digital product environments.

You have a track record of designing, implementing, and scaling A/B and multivariate tests that have generated measurable improvements in revenue, user activation, or conversion.

You’ve built with AI and agents yourself: Claude Code, automations, APIs, evals, creative pipelines. Show us something you built, not a certificate.

A Conversion Architect, skilled at diagnosing funnel bottlenecks and translating data into structured experimentation roadmaps that deliver measurable business outcomes.

A Behavioral Strategist, fascinated by what drives human action, able to mix cognitive psychology, UX, and persuasion frameworks to design experiences that convert because they resonate.

A Data-driven Creator, fluent in GA4, Hotjar, AB Tasty, VWO, Amplitude, not just running tests, but connecting insights to revenue, retention, and lifetime value.

Loop thinker: you see every channel as a loop that can learn, and you can’t stop yourself from closing it.

Taste: you know a great hook in two seconds, and you’d rather ship one great ad than ten average ones.

Numbers-honest: you say when a test failed. You care about real revenue, not vanity metrics.

Builder, not manager: you’d rather build the system than coordinate an agency.

#6956. Graduate Software Engineer, Open Source and Linux, Canonical Ubuntu at Canonical

We are hiring 2026 and 2027 Graduate Software Engineers into engineering teams around the world. As a global platform company we offer a wide range of software engineering challenges, in both open source products and commercial business systems. We have teams that work in a variety of languages, typically Python, Golang, Rust, C/C++, JavaScript and of course... Bash. We work from the very lowest levels of the Linux system - BIOS and boot loaders, firmware, kernel and drivers, all the way up to desktop applications and containers. We work on tiny devices like the Raspberry Pi and new RISC-V boards, all the way up to supercomputers, clouds and Kubernetes clusters. We work on compilers and toolchains, and we work on security, cryptography, performance and documentation. Some of our teams focus on packaging thousands of pieces of software, others focus on deep contributions to a single body of code.

If you are excited about the potential that open source has for humanity and the enterprise, you have outstanding results in your academic career, and you are excited to contribute to the open source stack, this is the best way to apply to Canonical. We will work with you to identify teams and projects that would be interesting and a good fit for your skills and motivations. The process is highly competitive, it will require effort and excellence to succeed if you apply here.

Most of our engineering teams work from home. We try to align a single team in a single time zone - EMEA (Europe, Middle East and Africa), APAC (Asia Pacific and Indian Ocean), and AMER (North, South and Central Americas). Even though you work from home, we try to ensure you always have colleagues alongside to coach and mentor you, and for the companionship of shared problem solving and pair programming. Larger projects have multiple teams and might span the globe. We work with the open source community and specialize in building teams that work well in the 'fishbowl' of open source engineering. This is full time work, five days a week, often solving hard problems with significant responsibility. We don't generally work weekends and nights and we do enjoy good holidays but we also expect productive and intense engagement Monday to Friday.

We bring all our engineering teams together in person twice a year for a deep 'sprint' with other teams to stimulate discussion and enable a wide range of career development paths. This is a fantastic way to demonstrate and develop your skill in software, learn about a wide range of technologies, make a meaningful impact to open source, and see some very interesting new cities that you might never otherwise get to visit.

If you find the open source stack amazing, and you have been a consistent high-performing student in your school and university, then this will be a challenging and exciting start to your career.

Location: This is a Globally remote role.

We consider geographical location, experience, and performance in shaping compensation worldwide. We assess performance and revisit compensation twice annually to ensure we recognize outstanding colleagues. In addition to base pay, we offer a performance-driven annual bonus or commission. We provide all team members with additional benefits which reflect our values and ideals. We balance our programs to meet local needs and ensure fairness globally.

Canonical is a pioneering tech firm at the forefront of the global move to open source. Our platform, Ubuntu, is very widely used in breakthrough enterprise initiatives such as public cloud, data science, AI, engineering innovation, and IoT. Our customers include the world's leading public cloud and silicon providers, and industry leaders in many sectors. We are founder-led, profitable, and growing.

Powered by worldwide distributed collaboration since our inception in 2004, we believe tech and business leadership can emerge anywhere. We have no central headquarters. Most of us work from home, but we are exceptionally disciplined about this. We have very high expectations of all colleagues, and are champions of excellence. Working here is a step into the future and will challenge you to think differently, work smarter, learn new skills, and raise your game.

We are proud to foster a workplace free from discrimination. Diversity of experience, perspectives, and background create a better work environment and better products. Whatever your identity, we will give your application fair consideration.

#6950. AI Native Creative Strategist - EdTech (French fluent 🇫🇷) at Paradox

What if your next playground was to build a global, iconic Brand, with a creative machine that never sleeps, and compounds?

We’re not looking for a "Social Media Manager" to follow a calendar. We’re looking for an AI-native Creative Strategist (Brand & Content).

Someone who understands that to win today, you don’t just need "content", you need a Movement.

This isn’t another content job. Most content teams use AI to write captions faster. That’s the 99%. We want to be in the 0.1%: a media where AI runs the creative loop, from the signal to the hook, the cut, the post and the lesson. Every day, on every platform, in French and in English. You bring the taste. The machine you build brings the scale.

Here’s the bet: a company that learns by itself grows in a way a company of people alone can’t. We aim for $10M in revenue with 10 people, each of them running a dozen agents.

Paradoxically, the more AI runs our content, the more human it has to feel. AI doesn’t make more of what everyone makes. It makes more of what only we can make.

1. Read the signals. Comments, DMs, retention graphs, saves, shares, search trends, objections from sales calls, and what the best creators and advertisers in the world are publishing right now.

2. Hold the full context of Paradox. Our DNA, years of interviews, lives and courses, member stories, and every past post with what it taught us, in one shared company brain.

3. Generate in our voice. Hooks, scripts, shorts, carousels, cuts, thumbnails and captions, in French and in English.

4. Ship. Format, schedule, publish and answer, on every platform.

5. Measure with real KPIs. Retention, shares, saves, follower growth, and the followers who become leads and members. Not likes.

6. Learn and loop on its own. Double down on what works, kill what doesn’t, write the lesson back into the brain, and start the next cycle without waiting for a meeting.

This is already real. An ads library of thousands of creatives feeds our creative research. Agents cut ads from long-form content and wire Instagram comment-to-DM automations. And our product is AI-native too: Athena, our AI coach, works with members every day.

At Paradox, we are convinced that greatness is trainable, and we believe self-help is broken.

Most learning platforms are used the same way: consuming generic theories, getting quick highs, ending with no real change.

People deserve more: a system that knows them, one that adapts, challenges, and grows with them.

That’s why we want to empower people at scale, by leveraging cutting edge technologies like AI, and crafting user experiences with outstanding aesthetics.

- Human Intelligence (coaches, peers, and mentors)

- Structured Intelligence (protocols, mental models, rituals)

- Technological Intelligence (AI personalization, feedback loops, adaptive journeys)

To create one seamless ecosystem: Paradox OS.

Paradox was founded by David Laroche, a creator followed by over 2 million people and known for his interviews with icons like Tony Parker, Mike Horn, Richard Branson and Gad Elmaleh. We started in French. We’re building for the world.

You lead the Content Factory not by management, but with creativity.

In a classic content team, people do the work and AI helps. Here it’s the other way round: agents do the volume, you design the machine, set the bar and make the calls. Humans do what no machine can: taste.

Build a symbiotic strategy where David Laroche’s personal brand and Paradox’s company brand reinforce each other without cannibalization.

Implement radical new formats (documentaries, conceptual shorts) that shatter the boring codes of the "Self-Help" industry.

Define distinct, sharp editorial lines for each platform: Depth & Search for YouTube, Aesthetic & Vibe for Instagram, and Authority & Authenticity for LinkedIn.

❌ The old way: a strategy deck written once a year, and a calendar filled by habit.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: agents watch every platform, every competitor and every comment continuously. They tell you which angle is rising, which format is tiring and which story our audience asks for. The strategy updates every week, on evidence.

Take responsibility for the entire production chain from hooks to storyboarding the narrative, and ensuring the final cut matches the vision.

Own the packaging: titles, thumbnails and the first 3 seconds. You test variants every week and know your click-through and retention numbers by heart.

❌ The old way: one shoot becomes one video and a handful of posts.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: one shoot, one live or one interview becomes dozens of hooks, shorts, carousels and ad cuts by the next morning, in both languages. Concepts are tested with AI-generated drafts before we spend a day shooting them.

Your taste becomes the eval. You write down what makes a great hook, a great cut, a great carousel and a great story, in rules and examples the agents check themselves against. Each time you reject something, the reason goes back into the rules.

Act as the final quality check: nothing gets published if it doesn’t meet the "Paradox Standard" of aesthetics and storytelling, whether a person or an agent made it.

❌ The old way: a creative team briefs an editor, waits three days and gets two versions.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: agents draft, cut, caption, translate and schedule. You keep the best, and next week their first drafts are already closer to your bar.

Analyze retention graphs, CTR, and CPL to iterate based on feedback, killing what fails and aggressively scaling what works.

❌ The old way: someone pulls the stats at the end of the month and nobody changes anything.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: agents read the numbers of every post every day, explain why it worked or didn’t, and propose the next test. You measure the machine itself: posts per week, time from idea to live, hit rate, and the share of the work done without a human.

We started in French. You build the machine that takes Paradox to English-speaking audiences: channels, formats and voice that feel native, not translated.

And always: real stories told with consent, no fake claims, no fake scarcity. The machine can scale, but it doesn’t decide what’s right. You do.

How you’ll know you’re AI-native: you spend your time on concepts, taste and calls, not on resizing, captioning and exporting. The output grows every month while the team doesn’t.

Proven Content Builder: You have successfully grown a media brand, a YouTube channel, or a personal brand to significant scale (100k+ followers or millions of views). You understand the mechanics of virality and retention. You’ve created at least one format that broke out, and you can show its click-through and retention numbers.

Elite Scripting Skills: You can show us scripts you wrote that performed. You know how to structure a narrative, write a hook that stops the scroll, and keep an audience engaged for 20+ minutes.

Performance Fluency: You have worked in environments where content had to drive business results (ads, launches, conversion), not just vanity metrics.

AI-native Creative: you’ve built content pipelines with AI yourself: Claude Code, automations, generative video, image and voice tools, evals. Show us something you built, not a certificate.

Intolerant to Mediocrity: You have "Taste." You are physically pained by a bad font, a clumsy cut, or a weak headline. You fight for the details that others ignore, including in what AI produces.

Psychology Obsessed: You don’t just "make content"; you study human nature. You constantly ask why people click, why they share, and what emotion drives them.

A "Doer" with Authority: You command respect not by title, but by competence. You can jump into a project, write the script, direct the shoot, and fix the edit if needed.

Loop thinker: you see every platform as a loop that can learn, and you can’t stop yourself from closing it.

No Ego, Just Impact: You don’t fall in love with your ideas. You accept that even the best concepts can flop. When it happens, you don’t take it personally; you analyze the data, pivot immediately, and ship the next concept with renewed energy.

English: Fluent English, you can hold a conversation on specific details with English-speaking colleagues.

Our chosen language for written communication is English.

❌ If you need clarity before chaos: wrong place.

❌ If you prefer balance over intensity: wrong season.

❌ If you don’t genuinely care about the topics we cover: wrong place.

❌ If you want to strategize without getting your hands dirty in scripting, directing, or the editing suite: wrong role.

❌ If you think AI content means generic content: wrong role. We use AI to multiply what only we can make.

❌ If you want to manage a large team: we’d rather build the machine than grow the headcount.

Help us market a product that fuses neuroscience, design, and AI to reinvent how humans grow.

Build one of the first truly AI-native media, and write the playbook others will copy.

In a classic content team, people do the work and AI helps. Here it’s the other way round: agents do the volume, you design the machine, set the bar and make the calls. Humans do what no machine can: taste.

Build a symbiotic strategy where David Laroche’s personal brand and Paradox’s company brand reinforce each other without cannibalization.

Implement radical new formats (documentaries, conceptual shorts) that shatter the boring codes of the "Self-Help" industry.

Define distinct, sharp editorial lines for each platform: Depth & Search for YouTube, Aesthetic & Vibe for Instagram, and Authority & Authenticity for LinkedIn.

❌ The old way: a strategy deck written once a year, and a calendar filled by habit.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: agents watch every platform, every competitor and every comment continuously. They tell you which angle is rising, which format is tiring and which story our audience asks for. The strategy updates every week, on evidence.

Take responsibility for the entire production chain from hooks to storyboarding the narrative, and ensuring the final cut matches the vision.

Own the packaging: titles, thumbnails and the first 3 seconds. You test variants every week and know your click-through and retention numbers by heart.

❌ The old way: one shoot becomes one video and a handful of posts.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: one shoot, one live or one interview becomes dozens of hooks, shorts, carousels and ad cuts by the next morning, in both languages. Concepts are tested with AI-generated drafts before we spend a day shooting them.

Your taste becomes the eval. You write down what makes a great hook, a great cut, a great carousel and a great story, in rules and examples the agents check themselves against. Each time you reject something, the reason goes back into the rules.

Act as the final quality check: nothing gets published if it doesn’t meet the "Paradox Standard" of aesthetics and storytelling, whether a person or an agent made it.

❌ The old way: a creative team briefs an editor, waits three days and gets two versions.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: agents draft, cut, caption, translate and schedule. You keep the best, and next week their first drafts are already closer to your bar.

Analyze retention graphs, CTR, and CPL to iterate based on feedback, killing what fails and aggressively scaling what works.

❌ The old way: someone pulls the stats at the end of the month and nobody changes anything.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: agents read the numbers of every post every day, explain why it worked or didn’t, and propose the next test. You measure the machine itself: posts per week, time from idea to live, hit rate, and the share of the work done without a human.

We started in French. You build the machine that takes Paradox to English-speaking audiences: channels, formats and voice that feel native, not translated.

And always: real stories told with consent, no fake claims, no fake scarcity. The machine can scale, but it doesn’t decide what’s right. You do.

How you’ll know you’re AI-native: you spend your time on concepts, taste and calls, not on resizing, captioning and exporting. The output grows every month while the team doesn’t.

Proven Content Builder: You have successfully grown a media brand, a YouTube channel, or a personal brand to significant scale (100k+ followers or millions of views). You understand the mechanics of virality and retention. You’ve created at least one format that broke out, and you can show its click-through and retention numbers.

Elite Scripting Skills: You can show us scripts you wrote that performed. You know how to structure a narrative, write a hook that stops the scroll, and keep an audience engaged for 20+ minutes.

Performance Fluency: You have worked in environments where content had to drive business results (ads, launches, conversion), not just vanity metrics.

AI-native Creative: you’ve built content pipelines with AI yourself: Claude Code, automations, generative video, image and voice tools, evals. Show us something you built, not a certificate.

Intolerant to Mediocrity: You have "Taste." You are physically pained by a bad font, a clumsy cut, or a weak headline. You fight for the details that others ignore, including in what AI produces.

Psychology Obsessed: You don’t just "make content"; you study human nature. You constantly ask why people click, why they share, and what emotion drives them.

A "Doer" with Authority: You command respect not by title, but by competence. You can jump into a project, write the script, direct the shoot, and fix the edit if needed.

Loop thinker: you see every platform as a loop that can learn, and you can’t stop yourself from closing it.

No Ego, Just Impact: You don’t fall in love with your ideas. You accept that even the best concepts can flop. When it happens, you don’t take it personally; you analyze the data, pivot immediately, and ship the next concept with renewed energy.

English: Fluent English, you can hold a conversation on specific details with English-speaking colleagues.

#6941. New Industry Alliance to Work Toward Common Security Model for AI Agents

Okta has announced a coalition bringing together major cloud, security, data and application vendors to create a shared architecture for securing AI agents across enterprise environments.

The 12 founding members of the new Blueprint Alliance are AWS, CrowdStrike, Databricks, Docker, Google Cloud, Lovable, Okta, Proofpoint, Salesforce, ServiceNow, Wiz and Zscaler. GE Appliances and World Central Kitchen are participating as strategic advisors.

The effort expands an earlier Okta framework into an open, multi-vendor reference architecture intended to address a basic problem with enterprise AI agents: An agent can be created in one system, authenticated through another, access tools and data from several others, and be monitored by yet another security platform.

The Alliance is focused less on how agents are built and more on how organizations can govern them consistently across vendor boundaries.

The Blueprint Alliance architecture organizes agent security around four questions: Where are my agents? What can they do? What are they doing? How do I respond?

Those questions translate into several practical areas:

The reference architecture also covers development-related systems. Its whitepaper includes agent frameworks, code repositories, CI/CD environments, code assistants, tool registries, Model Context Protocol servers, and command-line tools among the components that may need to be discovered or governed.

That makes the initiative relevant to development teams deploying agents that interact with internal APIs, MCP tools, databases, SaaS applications, or other agents. The architecture calls for those connections and permissions to remain visible and controllable as agents move from development into production.

A major part of the Alliance's work will be testing how security information and controls can move between products from different vendors.

The initial architecture references several existing standards and protocols, including MCP for agent-to-tool interactions, the Open Cybersecurity Schema Framework for security telemetry, and the Shared Signals Framework and Continuous Access Evaluation Profile for exchanging risk information.

The goal is for one part of an enterprise stack to be able to detect a risk condition and pass that information to another control point that can act on it. Depending on the implementation, that could include revoking access or containing an agent whose behavior has changed.

The Alliance says members will build and test reference integrations and publish results as interoperability work progresses. Specific cross-vendor implementations and performance results were not documented in the launch announcement.

Founding member Lovable also published a developer-oriented explanation of its participation, describing organizations that may have internally built agents, agents imported through SaaS platforms and locally running agents that all need to be inventoried and governed under a common model.

The Alliance's whitepaper recommends that organizations begin with foundational capabilities such as unified logging, telemetry, and agent discovery rather than attempting to deploy every control at once.

The architecture is available for community use, and the Alliance says it will continue evolving as members test integrations and additional participants become involved. Timelines for individual vendor integrations have not been published.

For more information, check out the Okta announcement here.

David Ramel is an editor and writer at Converge 360.

Cybersecurity company Flashpoint announced it has received U.S. Patent No. 12,705,360 for its Ransomware Risk model, a methodology designed to help security teams determine which newly disclosed vulnerabilities more closely resemble those historically used in ransomware attacks.

Cybercriminals are moving away from mass phishing scams and focusing on more targeted attacks, often using trusted websites and services to trick victims, according to a new report from KnowBe4.

District and state policies run the gamut from embracing to banning AI. This raises a key question: When does AI accelerate student learning and when does it interfere?

Ransomware attacks are becoming more automated, and AI could take that much further, according to cybersecurity company NCC Group's July 2026 threat intelligence report.

#6937. AI Platform Behind 12,000 E-mail Breaches Dismantled

The platform moved quickly. Since launching in February, EvilTokens had been tied to more than 12,000 compromised inboxes across over 10,000 organizations worldwide, according to Microsoft. Its victims included businesses and institutions in financial services, healthcare, construction, real estate, wholesale distribution, and higher education.

Microsoft and its partners seized 50 websites used to operate EvilTokens and disabled more than 150 additional domains supporting the service. The Metropolitan Police Service in the United Kingdom also arrested two men on Sept. 11 in connection with the alleged operation.

At the center of EvilTokens was an AI chatbot designed specifically for cybercrime. Once attackers gained access to an inbox, the chatbot could analyze messages, map relationships, identify employees involved in payments, and recommend ways to impersonate trusted contacts.

Microsoft said the platform helped criminals determine "who to target, who to impersonate," and how to get the most money from a compromised organization.

Business e-mail compromise has traditionally required attackers to manually comb through messages, learn how an organization handles payments, and find the right person to impersonate. However, EvilTokens simplified the entire process by automating much of that work.

The tools could quickly sort through a stolen inbox, summarize or translate messages, find invoices and wire-transfer discussions, and identify the people who moved money. EvilTokens could then draft messages that sounded like they came from the victim. It packaged all of this with mailbox access, phishing tools, management dashboards, and even customer support.

The service was sold through Telegram for a $1,500 startup fee, followed by a $500 monthly subscription. Microsoft's complaint also described a bulk e-mail tool that cost $600 a month and a more advanced e-mail delivery platform priced at $1,000 a month.

To break into a target, EvilTokens relied on device-code phishing. Victims were sent to a real Microsoft sign-in page and persuaded to enter an authentication code. The login process looked legitimate because it was, but the code quietly authorized a session controlled by the attacker.

That also made cleanup more complicated. The attackers stole OAuth tokens and authenticated sessions instead of passwords, so a password reset alone might not remove their access. Administrators also needed to revoke the stolen tokens and active sessions.

According to Microsoft's complaint,  EvilTokens used fake Microsoft 365 security notices and other branded templates to direct users into the device authentication flow. Once inside, attackers could use Microsoft Graph and other APIs to search and monitor a mailbox.

The company also found that AI played a role in creating EvilTokens itself. Investigators said much of the platform appeared to have been "vibe coded," with its developers using AI to quickly build and expand the service. EvilTokens reportedly drew on multiple AI models, including improperly integrated services from Groq and OpenAI.

The court authorized Microsoft to take control of or disable domains connected to the operation. Microsoft worked with Cloudflare, Coinbase, OpenAI, Railway, SpyCloud, the Shadowserver Foundation, and TRM Labs on the disruption. Health-ISAC joined the case because healthcare organizations were among the targets.

This marks the Microsoft Digital Crimes Unit's 40th court-authorized disruption and its first targeting an end-to-end, AI-enabled cybercrime service.

EvilTokens' infrastructure may be offline, but the model behind it is unlikely to disappear. The operation showed how AI can turn a stolen inbox into an immediately useful fraud playbook, allowing less-experienced criminals to launch attacks that once required considerable time and skill.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog post.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

#6931. Redesigned Microsoft Copilot Will Build Apps and Work on Its Own

Micorosft has announced that it's redesigning Copilot, the company's all-encompassing AI assistant, so users can hand off complex tasks, build tools by describing what they need, and assign work to an agent that keeps going after they log off.

The new Copilot experience has three main parts: Home, Code, and Autopilot. It also brings Word, Excel, and PowerPoint into the Copilot app. For users, the pitch is simple: Start a task in one place and stay there as it grows from a quick question into a document, an app, or a longer-running project. Most of the new capabilities are still rolling out or in preview.

Home puts Chat and Copilot Cowork together. Chat remains the place for quick answers, research, and drafts. Cowork handles work a user wants to delegate, such as preparing a briefing or pulling together a response to a request for proposals. Microsoft said Copilot eventually will route requests to Chat, Cowork, or Code on its own.

Office in Copilot will allow users to create a Word document, Excel workbook, or PowerPoint deck without leaving that experience. The files remain editable in the regular Office apps, and colleagues can work on them at the same time. Someone building a presentation, for example, could draw on a workbook, draft the slides, and keep refining both files from within Copilot.

Code is aimed at people who need a tool but might never open a development environment. A user can describe a tracker, dashboard, app, or workflow in plain language and have Copilot build it. Microsoft calls these "small, purpose-built solutions." The feature uses the same underlying technology as GitHub Copilot and runs in a sandboxed environment. A separate Copilot Managed Runtime, now in preview, lets organizations host and share the resulting apps within Microsoft 365.

Then there is Autopilot, previously known as Scout. It is designed for work that does not end with a single response: following a discussion, checking in with stakeholders, or keeping a project moving over several days. "Give it a name, a role, and a goal," Microsoft said. Autopilot runs in the cloud and has its own identity, memory, and workspace in the customer's tenant. Microsoft said its actions are subject to permissions, auditing, and organizational controls.

Those longer-running tasks also change how customers pay for Copilot. In a separate pricing post, Microsoft said everyday work in Chat and Microsoft 365 apps will remain covered by a per-user subscription. Cowork, Code, and Autopilot will be billed separately through usage-based Copilot Credits. A Copilot user subscription will still be needed to access those features.

For enterprise customers, the features that charge by usage will stay off until an admin sets up a spending policy in the Microsoft 365 admin center. Microsoft said there will be no charges before then. Admins can set budgets for the whole organization or for specific groups, limit how much individual users can spend, and get alerts when costs approach a set amount. Reports will show who is using the features and which tasks are driving costs.

Microsoft is bringing Code and Copilot Managed Runtime under those cost controls alongside Cowork. Copilot Studio agents are expected to follow in October. Before turning the features on for more employees, IT departments will need to decide who can use them, how much they can spend and which tasks are worth the extra cost.

Home and Code will begin rolling out through Microsoft's Frontier early access program in the coming weeks. Code's Frontier rollout starts at the end of September, when Autopilot also expands to private preview.

For more information, visit the Microsoft site.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

#6926. AI Leaders Warn of Risks at United Nations Security Council Briefing

The heads of OpenAI, Anthropic, and Hugging Face recently joined AI researcher Yoshua Bengio at the United Nations headquarters in New York on September 23 to warn the UN Security Council about the security risks of increasingly capable AI.

They did not offer a single plan for managing those risks. Bengio called for licensing and independent safety reviews. OpenAI's Sam Altman proposed common standards that countries could adopt through their own laws. Anthropic's Dario Amodei urged AI developers to slow down when safety work cannot keep pace. Hugging Face's Clément Delangue warned that limiting access to capable systems could leave defenders at a disadvantage.

France, which held the Security Council's rotating presidency, convened the meeting on AI and international security. The discussion exposed a difficult question for governments: how to oversee technology that crosses borders when countries disagree about who should set the rules.

Bengio, co-chair of the UN's Independent International Scientific Panel on AI, challenged the idea that competition forces companies to keep building more powerful systems. "The race is not a law of nature: it is the product of choices," he said in his published remarks. "They've told us they would slow down if they could. They can."

He called on developers to show independent experts that frontier systems are safe to train and deploy. He also proposed incident reporting, licensing, and liability insurance. Bengio identified catastrophic misuse, concentrated power, and the loss of human control as risks that require international cooperation.

Altman acknowledged the danger of moving too quickly. "We have unilaterally slowed down in the past. We will do so in the future," he said, according to remarks published by OpenAI. Decisions about AI's direction "cannot be made by labs in San Francisco alone," he added.

Altman proposed complementary national and international standards to measure frontier AI capabilities, assess risks, and test safeguards. He said countries should be able to compare evidence and verify commitments. He also called for faster reporting of serious incidents and secure channels for sharing emerging threats. Such standards should accommodate both open and closed models without favoring established companies, he said.

Amodei made a more direct commitment about the pace of Anthropic's releases: "We will slow down as much as necessary," he told the council. He identified two principal concerns: the misuse of AI to develop biological weapons and the possibility that advanced systems could outstrip developers' ability to control them.

The Anthropic chief proposed placing outside evaluators inside AI companies with access comparable to employees. He suggested that governments begin with a narrow agreement against using AI to make biological weapons, then develop ways to evaluate models and verify commitments across borders. He also called for common testing standards and a system for notifying other countries of significant AI incidents.

Delangue drew on a July incident in which Hugging Face disclosed an AI agent cyberattack. He called for stronger monitoring and disclosure, but argued that safety also depends on giving defenders access to capable tools. The central danger, he said, was "asymmetry of powerful AI" between attackers and defenders, large companies and everyone else, and wealthy countries and the rest of the world. His team used an open-source model while responding to the attack, he said.

The U.S. government opposed a broader international regulatory role. White House science adviser Michael Kratsios told the council that countries should develop their own ability to use and govern the technology. In the U.S. statement, he rejected new global governance structures and used the phrase "superintelligence, formerly known as AI," adopting President Donald Trump's terminology from his General Assembly speech the previous day.

The four AI briefers differed on how far oversight should go and how widely capable systems should be available. They agreed on a narrower point: Governments need better ways to understand what advanced AI systems can do and to detect when safeguards fail.

#6916. OpenAI Getting Rid of Custom GPTs and Moving to Plugin Architecture

OpenAI has announced it will retire Custom GPTs and move the specialized workflows built around them to a new plugin architecture spanning ChatGPT and its Codex coding agent.

Custom GPT retirement is currently scheduled for Dec. 11, 2026, although the company says timing and migration availability can vary by account and workspace.

For affected Enterprise workspaces, OpenAI's current migration schedule targets Oct. 26 for ending creation of new Custom GPTs. Qualifying Enterprise customers with approved deferrals can continue using them until Feb. 11, 2027.

OpenAI has changed its UI to make GPTs less prominent, and when users choose to try creating a new Custom GPT, they are now prompted with an option to create a plugin instead.

Rather than maintaining separate customized versions of ChatGPT, OpenAI is shifting customization toward plugins that can package reusable instructions, reference material, and connections to outside services.

Under OpenAI's plugin architecture, a plugin can include one or more skills containing instructions and resources for repeatable workflows, a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server exposing tools and external systems, or both. ChatGPT and Codex share a universal plugin directory, although some capabilities can remain specific to a particular environment.

For organizations, that makes the change more than a simple renaming exercise. A company could package recurring workflows into skills while using MCP servers or connected apps to give those workflows access to enterprise systems and data.

OpenAI says creators will be able to migrate an existing GPT through the My GPTs interface. The GPT's instructions become a skill in the replacement plugin, while knowledge files are copied into plugin reference files and connected apps can be carried over.

Not everything transfers automatically. Existing GPT conversations do not migrate, the selected model does not carry over, and Custom Actions may have to be rebuilt using an available app or a custom MCP server.

Sharing also changes. Migrated plugins start private, existing GPT users do not automatically receive access to the replacement, and prior sharing settings do not carry over.

OpenAI is also cautioning organizations against assuming that migration will preserve behavior exactly. "A migrated plugin may respond differently," the company says, recommending that users retest familiar prompts, reference-material use, required output formats, and integrations before relying on the replacement.

That warning has contributed to pushback from some GPT builders, who argue that plugins do not fully reproduce the dedicated and predictable environment of a Custom GPT. Other complaints have focused on sharing, migration complexity, and differences in how migrated workflows use instructions and reference material.

Despite those concerns, OpenAI is clearly consolidating around plugins as its common mechanism for extending its AI products. Its developer documentation now describes plugins as packages users can "discover, install, share, and publish" across ChatGPT and Codex, replacing the separate-custom-assistant model with reusable AI capabilities that can be combined with enterprise tools and services.

For more information, go to the OpenAI site.

David Ramel is an editor and writer at Converge 360.
